Simple Vectorial Lie Algebras in Characteristic 2 and their Superizations This paper is a contribution to the Special Issue on Algebra, Topology, and Dynamics in Interaction in honor of Dmitry Fuchs. The full collection is available at https://www.emis.de/journals/SIGMA/Fuchs.html
Sofiane BOUARROUDJ † 1 {}^{{\dagger}^{1}}, Pavel GROZMAN † 2 {}^{{\dagger}^{2}}, Alexei LEBEDEV † 2 {}^{{\dagger}^{2}}, Dimitry LEITES † 1 † 3 {}^{{\dagger}^{1}{\dagger}^{3}} and Irina SHCHEPOCHKINA † 4 {}^{{\dagger}^{4}}

Abstract
We overview the classifications of simple finite-dimensional modular Lie algebras. In characteristic 2, their list is wider than that in other characteristics; e.g., it contains desuperizations of modular analogs of complex simple vectorial Lie superalgebras. We consider odd parameters of deformations. For all 15 Weisfeiler gradings of the 5 exceptional families, and one Weisfeiler grading for each of 2 serial simple complex Lie superalgebras (with 2 exceptional subseries), we describe their characteristic-2 analogs – new simple Lie algebras. Descriptions of several of these analogs, and of their desuperizations, are far from obvious. One of the exceptional simple vectorial Lie algebras is a previously unknown deform (the result of a deformation) of the characteristic-2 version of the Lie algebra of divergence-free vector fields; this is a new simple Lie algebra with no analogs in characteristics distinct from 2. In characteristic 2, every simple Lie superalgebra can be obtained from a simple Lie algebra by one of the two methods described in arXiv:1407.1695. Most of the simple Lie superalgebras thus obtained from simple Lie algebras we describe here are new.



keywords
modular vectorial Lie algebra; modular vectorial Lie superalgebra

1
Notation and background

To make the text understandable for the uninitiated, we place the most basic facts before the Introduction which we have divided into two parts to make it more readable; for the same reason we divided the background to accommodate both students and experts. Statements proved directly, or by means of
Mathematica
-based
SuperLie
package
[
Gr
]
, are called Claims.

We recall the basics and show how to modify familiar
formulas in order to pass from
{\mathbb{C}}
to fields of positive characteristic, especially
characteristic 2. In some formulas given for
p=2
, we retain notation
convenient for comparison with the cases where
p\neq 2
.

1.1
Main points

1.

We give an overview of the classification of simple finite-dimensional modular Lie algebras and Lie superalgebras over an algebraically closed field
{\mathbb{K}}
of characteristic
p>0
. We update the conjectures for various values of
p>0
.

2.

We use our results on classification of Lie superalgebras of vector fields with polynomial coefficients over
{\mathbb{C}}
to describe their characteristic-
p
versions, especially, their desuperizations, for all 15 Weisfeiler gradings of all 5 exceptional simple vectorial Lie superalgebras, and for several serial ones, also exceptional in a sense.

3.

One of the deforms
1
1

1

Deform
is the result of a deformation, like transform is the result of a transformation.
of the divergence-free Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{svect}}(5;\underline{N})
which exists only if
p=2
. It is one of the exceptional simple vectorial Lie algebras – a desuperization of an exceptional simple vectorial Lie superalgebra.
This is the most unexpected result of this paper
.

1.2
Generalities

As is now customary, we denote the elements of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
by
{\bar{0}}
and

{\bar{1}}
, to distinguish them from integers. For us,
{\mathbb{N}}:=\{1,2,\dots\}
, as it used to be in the past, and still is in some countries; we set
{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}:={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
. The parity
p
of a non-zero element
v
of a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded space
V
, called a
superspace
, is equal to
i
if and only if
v\in V_{i}
. Any
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded algebra is called a
superalgebra
.

Hereafter
{\mathbb{K}}
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic

p>0
; usually,
p=2
and all Lie (super)algebras are finite-dimensional
unless otherwise stated.
Mostly (exceptions indicated),
\Pi
denotes the change of parity functor, i.e., tensoring by

\Pi({\mathbb{Z}})
.

The superization of most formulas of algebra is achieved via the following
sign rule

“If something of parity
a
is moved past something of parity
b
, the sign
(-1)^{ab}
accrues. Formulas defined only on homogeneous elements are extended to arbitrary elements via linearity.”

Note that only even homomorphisms are considered as

morphisms of superalgebras
.

Observe that sometimes applying the Sign Rule requires some dexterity. For example,
we have to distinguish between two versions both of which turn in the
non-super case into one, called skew- or anti-commutativity, which are synonyms only in the
non-super
case; for two elements
a
and
b
of a superalgebra we call the following conditions

\displaystyle ba=(-1)^{p(b)p(a)}ab\quad

\displaystyle\text{super commutativity},

\displaystyle ba=-(-1)^{p(b)p(a)}ab\quad

\displaystyle\text{super anti-commutativity},

\displaystyle ba=(-1)^{(p(b)+1)(p(a)+1)}ab\quad

\displaystyle\text{super skew-commutativity},

\displaystyle ba=-(-1)^{(p(b)+1)(p(a)+1)}ab\quad

\displaystyle\text{super antiskew-commutativity}.

Examples: the bracket in any Lie superalgebra is
super anti-commutative
; the anti-bracket
\{-,-\}_{\rm B.b.}
, see (
2.5
), being anti-commutative relative the parity in the Lie superalgebra is, however,

super antiskew-commutative
relative to the
natural parity of generating functions
.

1.2.1
Conventions and notation often used

In what follows, we assume that every
supercommutative
superalgebra is associative with 1; their morphisms send 1 to 1.

We denote by
{\mathfrak{c}}
the center of a given Lie (super)algebra; both

{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{g}})
and
{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{g}}:={\mathfrak{c}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}
denote a trivial central extension of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Let
{\mathfrak{a}}\ltimes{\mathfrak{b}}

or
{\mathfrak{b}}\rtimes{\mathfrak{a}}
denote the semi-direct sum of modules (algebras)
in which
{\mathfrak{a}}
is a submodule (ideal).

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{g}}):={\mathfrak{g}}\ltimes{\mathbb{K}}D
, where
D
is an outer derivation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
; unless specified otherwise,
D
is the grading operator of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. For example, for
{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{o}}_{\Pi}(2k))
, we take
D=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}\nolimits(I_{k},0_{k})
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}k}:=[{\mathfrak{g}},[{\mathfrak{g}},\dots[{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}]\dots]]
, the
k
-fold bracket.

We denote the
functor of raising to the
n
th symmetric (resp. exterior) power by

S^{n}
(resp.
E^{n}
, often denoted also by
\Lambda^{n}
); sometimes we denote the exterior (Grassmann)
algebra by
\Lambda[\theta]
or
\Lambda(r)
ingeneratorsθ
=(
θ
_1,…,
θ
_r)
satisfyinganti-commutativityrelations(and,additionally,
θ
_i^2=0
forall
i
if
p=2
).\par Thesymbol

id

(sometimes

id
_n
,

id
_a—b
){\@index{\@indexphrase[$Zmi$, $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{n}$, $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{a|b}$, the identity operator or the tautological module]{$\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits$, $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{n}$, $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{a|b}$, the identity operator\newline
or the tautological module}}}denotesnotonlytheidentityoperator(inthespaceofdimension
n
,resp.\ superspaceofsuperdimension
a—b
),butalsothetautologicalmodule
V
overthelinearLiesuperalgebra
g
⊂
gl
(V)
;sometimeswewrite

id
_
g
:=V
forclarity.\par
L_D
istheLiederivativealongthevectorfield~
D
.

1.2.2
Definition of Lie superalgebras for
\boldsymbol{p\neq 2,3}

The “naive” definition of Lie superalgebras for
p\neq 2,3
is obtained by applying the Sign Rule to anti-commutativity and Jacobi identities. To understand deformations with odd parameter, we need a more sophisticated approach using the functor of points. The multiplication in the Lie superalgebra will be called
super-bracket
or just
bracket
.

1.2.3
Definition of Lie superalgebras for
\boldsymbol{p=2}

If
p=2
, the
antisymmetry
condition for Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
should be replaced by an equivalent for
p\neq 2
, but otherwise
stronger,
alternating
or
antisymmetry
condition

[x,x]=0\ \text{for any $x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}$}.

If
p=2
, a
Lie superalgebra

is a superspace
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}
such that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
is a
Lie algebra,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}
is a
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
-module (made two-sided by
symmetry), with a
squaring

x\mapsto x^{2}
and a
bracket
of odd elements, which are defined via a linear map
s\colon S^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}})\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
, where
S^{2}
denotes the operator of raising to symmetric square, as follows:

\displaystyle x^{2}:=s(x\otimes x),

\displaystyle{}[x,y]:=s(x\otimes y+y\otimes x)\text{~for any $x,y\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}$.}

The linearity of the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
-valued function
s
implies that

(ax)^{2}=a^{2}x^{2}
for any
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}
and
a\in{\mathbb{K}}
, and

\displaystyle{}[x,y]\ \text{is a~bilinear form on ${\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}$ with values in ${\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}$.}

The
Jacobi identity
involving odd elements takes the form of the following two conditions:

\displaystyle\big[x^{2},y\big]=[x,[x,y]]\ \text{for any~}x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}},y\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}},\quad\big[x^{2},x\big]=0\ \text{for any $x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}$.}

(1.1)

The (super)algebra satisfying only Jacobi identity, without any symmetry conditions, is called a
Leibniz
(
super
)
algebra
.

Over
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
, the condition (
1.1
) must (for a reason, see
[
KLLS
]
) be replaced with a more general one:

\displaystyle\big[x^{2},y\big]=[x,[x,y]]\quad\text{for any $x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}$ and $y\in{\mathfrak{g}}$}.

(1.2)

For any other ground field, this condition is equivalent to condition (
1.1
).

More generally, for any Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, since we want the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{der}}\ {\mathfrak{g}}
of all derivations of
{\mathfrak{g}}
to be a Lie superalgebra, we have to add (to the Leibniz rule) the following condition on derivations (it becomes (
1.2
) for
D=\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{y}
)

D\big(x^{2}\big)=[D(x),x]\ \text{for odd elements $x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}$ and any $D\in{\mathfrak{der}}\ {\mathfrak{g}}$.}

By an
ideal

{\mathfrak{i}}
of a Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, one always means
{\mathfrak{i}}

homogeneous
with respect to parity, i.e., equal to
{{\mathfrak{i}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}\bigoplus{\mathfrak{i}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}}
; for
p=2
, the ideal should be closed with respect to squaring.

Recall that a given Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
is said to be

simple
if
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}>1
and
{\mathfrak{g}}
has no proper ideals;
{\mathfrak{g}}

is
semisimple
if its radical is zero;
{\mathfrak{g}}
is

almost simple
if it can be sandwiched (non-strictly) between
a simple Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{s}}
and the Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{der}}({\mathfrak{s}})
of derivations of
{\mathfrak{s}}
, i.e.,

{\mathfrak{s}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{der}}({\mathfrak{s}})
.

The definition of the
derived of the Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{g}}
changes when
p=2
: let
{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}:={\mathfrak{g}}
and for any
i\geq 0
, set

{\mathfrak{g}}^{(i+1)}=({\mathfrak{g}}^{(i)})^{(1)}:=\begin{cases}\big[{\mathfrak{g}}^{(i)},{\mathfrak{g}}^{(i)}\big]&\text{if $p\neq 2$},\\
\big[{\mathfrak{g}}^{(i)},{\mathfrak{g}}^{(i)}\big]+\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits\big\{g^{2}\,|\,g\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{(i)}_{\bar{1}}\big\}&\text{if $p=2$}.\end{cases}

An even linear mapping
r\colon{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
is said to be a

representation of the Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
V
is said to be a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module
if

\displaystyle r([x,y])=[r(x),r(y)]\quad\text{for any $x,y\in{\mathfrak{g}}$,}

\displaystyle r(x^{2})=(r(x))^{2}\quad\text{for any $x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}$.}

1.2.4
Definition of Lie superalgebras for
\boldsymbol{p=3}

Since we are giving a review of the context for any
p>0
, we have to note a peculiarity of

p=3
, where the
Jacobi identity
for
Lie superalgebras
entails, additionally, that

[x,[x,x]]=0\quad\text{for any $x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}$}.

(1.3)

The super anti-commutative algebra satisfying the Jacobi identity, but not (
1.3
) is called a
pre-Lie superalgebra
. For interesting examples of pre-Lie superalgebras, see
[
BBH
]
.

1.3
Basics on the functor of points

In this subsection, we follow
[
Ma
]
; we advise the reader interested in subtleties that we, like most authors, do not dwell on, to read the Appendices to
[
Mo
]
.

For a fixed object
M
and any object
X
of a category
{\sf C}
, the association
X\longmapsto\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\sf C}(X,M)
defines a functor
F\colon{\sf C}\leadsto\mathop{\mathrm{{\sf Sets}}}\nolimits
. The idea is

(A) To consider
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\sf C}(X,M)
as the set of points of
M
, which is indeed the case for any set
M
if
X
is a point of
M
, and
{\sf C}=\mathop{\mathrm{{\sf Sets}}}\nolimits
;

(B) Considering objects of the category
{\sf C}
of sets endowed with a structure (of a group, algebra, module over a fixed algebra, topological space, etc.), and the morphisms in
{\sf C}
being the maps of these sets preserving (exactly, or up to an equivalence, see
[
Ma
, Section 1.16.3]
) a certain structure (that of a group, or of an associative algebra, or of Lie algebra, etc.), as a model, we’d like to imitate these sets-with-a-structure by objects of another category.

For example, a
Lie supergroup
is any group in the category of supermanifolds, see
[
Ma
]
.

Likewise, a
Lie superalgebra
is any Lie algebra in the category of linear supervarieties, see
[
KLLS
]
. There we reformulate the naive definition of Lie superalgebras, which are
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded linear spaces with multiplication satisfying certain identities, in terms of supervarieties.

1.3.1
PBW-theorem for Lie superalgebras

In
[
Et
]
, an interesting description of conditions when the Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt theorem for Lie superalgebras holds (or not) is offered for
p>0
. Note, although we will not use this in this paper, that for Lie superalgebras understood “naively”, the PBW theorem holds.

1.3.2
Deformations of the brackets

Let
C
be a supercommutative superalgebra, let
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits C
be the affine super scheme.

Recall, see
[
Ru
]
, where the non-super case is considered, that a
deformation
of
a Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
over
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits C
, is a Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{G}}
such that
{\mathfrak{G}}\simeq{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes C
, as superspaces. The deformation is
trivial
if
{\mathfrak{G}}\simeq{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes C
, as Lie superalgebras, not just as superspaces, and
non-trivial
otherwise.

Generally, the
deforms
of
a Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
over
{\mathbb{K}}
are Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{G}}\otimes_{I}{\mathbb{K}}
, where
I
is any closed point in
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits C
.

In particular, consider a deformation with an odd parameter
\tau
. This is a Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{G}}
isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[\tau]
as a
super space
; if, moreover,
{\mathfrak{G}}\simeq{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[\tau]
as a
Lie superalgebra
, i.e.,

[a\otimes f,b\otimes g]=(-1)^{p(f)p(b)}[a,b]\otimes fg\quad\text{for all $a,b\in{\mathfrak{g}}$ and $f,g\in{\mathbb{K}}[\tau]$},

then the deformation is considered
trivial
(and
non-trivial
otherwise). Observe that
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\tau
is not an ideal of
{\mathfrak{G}}
: the ideal should be a free
{\mathbb{K}}[\tau]
-module.

Comment.
Consider formal deformations over
{\mathbb{K}}[[\tau]]
. If
the formal series in
\tau
converges in a domain
D
, we can evaluate
\tau
for any
\tau\in D
and – if
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}<\infty
– consider copies
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tau}
, where
\tau\in D
, of the same dimension as
{\mathfrak{g}}
. If the parameter is formal or odd, such an evaluation is possible only trivially:
\tau\mapsto 0
.

1.4
Linear (matrix) Lie superalgebras

Certain basics of linear superalgebra are not well-known, or given wrongly in the literature; no harm in recalling about them.

The
general linear
Lie
superalgebra of all supermatrices of size
Size
corresponding to linear operators in the superspace
V=V_{\bar{0}}\oplus V_{\bar{1}}
over the ground field
{\mathbb{K}}
is denoted by

{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})
, where
\text{Size}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{|\text{Size}|})
is an ordered
collection of parities of the basis vectors of
V
for which we take only vectors
homogeneous with respect to parity
and
|\text{Size}|:=\dim V
;
usually, for the
standard
(simplest from a certain point of view) format,
{\mathfrak{gl}}({\bar{0}},\dots,{\bar{0}},{\bar{1}},\dots,{\bar{1}})
is abbreviated to
{\mathfrak{gl}}(\dim V_{\bar{0}}|\dim V_{\bar{1}})
. Any supermatrix from
{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})
can
be uniquely expressed as the sum of its even and odd parts; in the
standard format this is the following block expression; on non-zero summands the parity is defined:

\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}$}=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&D\end{pmatrix}$}+\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\
C&0\end{pmatrix}$},\qquad p\left(\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&D\end{pmatrix}$}\right)={\bar{0}},\qquad p\left(\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\
C&0\end{pmatrix}$}\right)={\bar{1}}.

The
supertrace
is the map
{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})\longrightarrow{\mathbb{K}}
,

(X_{ij})\longmapsto\sum(-1)^{p_{i}(p(X)+1)}X_{ii}
.
Thus, in the standard format,
\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits A-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits D
.
Observe that for the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{\mathcal{C}}(p|q)
over a supercommutative superalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}
, i.e., for supermatrices with elements in
{\mathcal{C}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits X=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits A-(-1)^{p(X)}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits D\text{~~for any $X=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}$,}

\displaystyle\text{where $p(X)=p(A_{ij})=p(D_{kl})=p(B_{il})+{\bar{1}}=p(C_{kj})+{\bar{1}}$}.

So if
{\mathcal{C}}_{\bar{1}}\neq 0
, then on odd supermatrices the supertrace coincides with the trace.

Since
\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits\ [x,y]=0
, the subsuperspace of supertraceless
matrices constitutes a Lie subsuperalgebra of
{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})
called
special linear
and denoted

{\mathfrak{sl}}(\text{Size})
.

1.4.1
The queer version of
\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{gl}}(n)}

There are at least two super versions of
{\mathfrak{gl}}(n)
, not
one; for reasons, see
[
Lsos
, Chapters 1 and 7]
. The other version –
{\mathfrak{q}}(n)
– is called the
queer

Lie superalgebra and is defined as the one that preserves – if
p\neq 2
– the
complex structure given by an
odd
operator
J
, i.e.,

{\mathfrak{q}}(n)
is the centralizer
C(J)
of
J
:

{\mathfrak{q}}(n)=C(J)=\{X\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|n)\,|\,[X,J]=0\},\quad\text{where }J^{2}=-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits.

It is clear that by a change of basis we can reduce
J
to the form (shape)

J_{2n}

in the standard
format, and then
{\mathfrak{q}}(n)
takes the form

{\mathfrak{q}}(n)=\left\{(A,B):=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B&A\end{pmatrix},\ \text{where $A,B\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(n)$ and $J_{2n}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1_{n}\\
-1_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}$}\right\}.

(Over any algebraically closed field
{\mathbb{K}}
, instead of
J
we can take any odd operator
K
such that
K^{2}=a\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{n|n}
, where
a\in{\mathbb{K}}^{\times}
; and the Lie superalgebras
C(K)
are isomorphic for distinct
K
; if
p=2
, it is natural to select
K^{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
, and hence
\Pi_{n|n}:=\Pi_{2n}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1_{n}\\
1_{n}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
can serve as the normal shape of
K
.)

On
{\mathfrak{q}}(n)
, the supertrace vanishes, but the
queertrace
is defined:
\mathop{\mathrm{qtr}}\nolimits\colon(A,B)\longmapsto\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits B
. Denote by
{\mathfrak{sq}}(n)
the Lie superalgebra
of
queertraceless
matrices; set
{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{sq}}(n):={\mathfrak{sq}}(n)/{\mathbb{K}}1_{2n}
.

If
p=2
, on
\mathfrak{q}(n)
there is another (even) trace
{\text{htr}}\colon(A,B)\mapsto\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits(A)
, see
[
KLLS
]
.

1.4.2
The supermatrix of the dual operator (after
[
Lsos
]
)

Let
F\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits_{{\mathcal{C}}}(V)
. The passage from the matrix of
F
in a basis of
V
to the matrix of
F^{*}
in the dual basis of
V^{*}
is performed by means of the
supertransposition
which in the standard format is of the shape

X=\begin{pmatrix}R&S\\
T&U\end{pmatrix}\mapsto X^{\rm st}:=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}R^{\rm t}&T^{\rm t}\\
-S^{\rm t}&U^{\rm t}\end{pmatrix}&\text{if $p(X)={\bar{0}}$},\\
\begin{pmatrix}R^{\rm t}&-T^{\rm t}\\
S^{\rm t}&U^{\rm t}\end{pmatrix}&\text{if $p(X)={\bar{1}}$},\end{cases}

where
M^{\rm t}
is the transposed of the matrix
M
.

1.4.3
Lie superalgebras preserving bilinear forms

The supermatrices
X\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})
such that

X^{\rm st}B+(-1)^{p(X)p(B)}BX=0\quad\text{for an homogeneous matrix
$B\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})$}

constitute the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{aut}}({\mathcal{B}})
that preserves the
bilinear form
{\mathcal{B}}
on
V
whose Gram matrix
B=(B_{ij})
is given by the formula

\displaystyle B_{ij}=(-1)^{p(B)p(v_{i})}{\mathcal{B}}(v_{i},v_{j})\quad\text{for the basis vectors $v_{i}\in V$.}

(1.4)

In order to identify a bilinear form
B(V,W)
with an operator, an element of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W^{*})
, the matrix
B
of the bilinear form
{\mathcal{B}}
is defined in
[
Lsos
, Chapter 1]
by equation (
1.4
), not by
seemingly
natural – but inappropriate for such an identification – formula

\displaystyle B_{ij}={\mathcal{B}}(v_{i},v_{j})\quad\text{for the basis vectors $v_{i}\in V$.}

(1.5)

Moreover, the would-be definition (
1.5
) contradicts the manifest symmetry of the odd bilinear form
\mathop{\mathrm{qtr}}\nolimits
on
{\mathfrak{q}}(n)
. To correctly define symmetry of bilinear forms, consider the
upsetting
of bilinear forms

u\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Bil}}\nolimits(V,W)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Bil}}\nolimits(W,V)
, see
[
Lsos
, Chapter 1]
, given by the formula

\displaystyle u({\mathcal{B}})(w,v)=(-1)^{p(v)p(w)}{\mathcal{B}}(v,w)\quad\text{for any $v\in V$ and $w\in W$.}

If
V=W
, we say that

{\mathcal{B}}
is
symmetric
if

\displaystyle u(B)=B,\quad\text{where $u(B)=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}R^{\rm t}&(-1)^{p(B)}T^{\rm t}\\
(-1)^{p(B)}S^{\rm t}&-U^{\rm t}\end{pmatrix}$}$ for $B=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}R&S\\
T&U\end{pmatrix}$}$.}

Similarly,
{\mathcal{B}}
is
anti-symmetric
if
u(B)=-B
.

1.4.4
Notational convention

By abuse of notation we will often denote the bilinear form
{\mathcal{B}}
by its Gram matrix
B
in a normal shape.

1.5
Analogs of polynomials for
\boldsymbol{p>0}

Let
{\mathbb{C}}[x]:={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{a}]
denote the supercommutative superalgebra of polynomials
in indeterminates
x
in their
standard order
; i.e., let the
first
m
indeterminates be even and the other
n
be odd
(
m+n=a
). Among the bases of
{\mathbb{C}}[x]
in which the structure
constants are integers, the two bases are usually considered: the monomial
basis and the basis of
divided powers
constructed as follows.

For any multi-index
\underline{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{a})
, where

r_{1},\dots,r_{m}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
and
r_{m+1},\dots,r_{a}\in\{0,1\}
, we set

\displaystyle u_{i}^{(r_{i})}:=\frac{x_{i}^{r_{i}}}{r_{i}!}\quad\text{and}\quad u^{(\underline{r})}:=\prod\limits_{1\leq i\leq a}u_{i}^{(r_{i})}.

(1.6)

Clearly, we have

\displaystyle u^{(\underline{r})}\cdot u^{(\underline{s})}=\left(\mathop{\prod}\limits_{m+1\leq i\leq a}\min(1,2-r_{i}-s_{i})\cdot(-1)^{\mathop{\sum}\limits_{m<i<j\leq a}r_{j}s_{i}}\right)\cdot\Big(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{\underline{r} + \underline{s}}{\underline{r}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{\underline{r} + \underline{s}}{\underline{r}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\underline{r} + \underline{s}}{\underline{r}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\underline{r} + \underline{s}}{\underline{r}}$}}\Big)u^{(\underline{r}+\underline{s})},

(1.7)

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\Big(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{\underline{r} + \underline{s}}{\underline{r}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{\underline{r} + \underline{s}}{\underline{r}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\underline{r} + \underline{s}}{\underline{r}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\underline{r} + \underline{s}}{\underline{r}}$}}\Big):=\mathop{\prod}\limits_{1\leq i\leq m}\Big(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{r_{i}
+ s_{i}}{r_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{r_{i}
+ s_{i}}{r_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{r_{i}
+ s_{i}}{r_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{r_{i}
+ s_{i}}{r_{i}}$}}\Big).

These
u_{i}^{(r_{i})}
form an “integer basis” (i.e., a basis in which all structure constants with respect to the product (
1.7
) are integers) of
{\mathbb{C}}[x]
.

1.5.1
Notational convention

In what follows, for clarity, we will write exponents of divided powers in parentheses.

Over any field
{\mathbb{K}}
of characteristic
p>0
, we consider the
supercommutative superalgebra (now we do not have any elements
x
, only the
u_{i}^{(r_{i})}
)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}(m;\underline{N}|n):={\mathbb{K}}[u;\underline{N}]:=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{K}}}\left(u^{(\underline{r})}\,|\,r_{i}\begin{cases}<p^{N_{i}}&\text{for $i\leq m$}\\
=0\text{ or 1}&\text{for $i>m$}\end{cases}\right),

with multiplication given by formula (
1.7
)
where
\underline{N}=(N_{1},\dots,N_{m})
is the
shearing
vector
with
N_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}\cup\infty
(we assume that

p^{\infty}=\infty
). Important particular cases of shearing vectors:

\displaystyle\mathbbmss{1}:=(1,\dots,1)\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{N}_{\infty}:=(\infty,\dots,\infty);\quad\text{we set~}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}(m):={\mathcal{O}}(m;\underline{N}_{\infty}).

(1.8)

The algebra

{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{O}}(m;\underline{N}|n)={\mathbb{K}}[u;\underline{N}]\quad\text{and its completion $\widehat{\mathcal{O}}(m;\underline{N}_{\infty}|n)$}

(1.9)

are called the
algebras of
divided powers
. We will sometimes need completion
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}\big(\widehat{}\underline{N}_{i}\big)
with respect to only one indeterminate, where
\big(\widehat{}\underline{N}_{i}\big)_{j}=\underline{N}_{j}
except for
\big(\widehat{}\underline{N}_{i}\big)_{i}=\infty
.

Clearly,
{\mathcal{O}}(a;\mathbbmss{1})={\mathbb{K}}[u;\mathbbmss{1}]
is the algebra of truncated
polynomials. Only
{\mathbb{K}}[u;\mathbbmss{1}]
is indeed generated by the
declared indeterminates whereas the list of generators of
{\mathbb{K}}[u;\underline{N}]

consists of
u_{i}^{(p^{k_{i}})}
for all
i
and all
k_{i}
such that

0\leq k_{i}<N_{i}
if
u_{i}
is even.

1.6
The (generalized) Cartan prolongation

Let

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{-d\leq i\leq-1}{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
be a nilpotent

{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie (super)algebra and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
a Lie sub(super)algebra of the Lie
(super)algebra
\mathfrak{der}_{0}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-})
of degree 0 derivations of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}
.
Recall that the graded Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{b}}=\oplus_{p\geqslant-d}\ {\mathfrak{b}}_{p}

is said to be
transitive
if for all
p\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\{x\in{\mathfrak{b}}_{p}\,|\,[x,{\mathfrak{b}}_{-}]=0\}=0,\quad\text{where $\mathfrak{b}_{-}:=\oplus_{i<0}\mathfrak{b}_{i}$}.

The maximal transitive
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie (super)algebra whose non-positive part is
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is called the (generalized)
Cartan prolong
of the pair
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})
and is denoted by
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}
.

If
p=0
, we can realize
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}
by elements of negative degree of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m;\vec{r})
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
by elements of 0th degree of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m;\vec{r})
in a non-standard (see Section
2.4.1
) grading of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)
, where
n|m={\rm sdim}{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}
. Then the Cartan prolong
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}:=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{k\geq-d}{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}

of the pair
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})
is obtained for any
k>0
by

{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}:=\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m;\vec{r})_{k}\,|\,[D,{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}]\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{k+i}\text{~~for any $i<0$}\}.

The above-described procedure is called
generalized
prolongation because the initial Cartan prolongation was defined for
d=1
only.

1.6.1
Partial Cartan prolongation involving positive components

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
be
a proper
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-submodule such that
[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}]={\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
. If such

{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
exists (usually,
[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}]\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
), define
the 2nd prolongation of
(\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{i\leq 0}{\mathfrak{g}}_{i})\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
to be

{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}:=\{D\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}\,|\,[D,{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}]\subset{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\}.

The terms
{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}
, where
i>2
, are similarly defined. Set

{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}:={\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
for
i\leq 0
and call
{\mathfrak{h}}_{*}:=\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}
the
partial Cartan prolong involving positive components
.

Examples.
The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|n;n)
is a subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2n;n)
. The former is obtained as the Cartan prolong of the same
nonpositive part as
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2n;n)
and a submodule of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2n;n)_{1}
. The simple exceptional superalgebra
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
discovered in

[
Sh5
,
Sh14
]
is another example.

1.7
Vectorial Lie algebras and algebras of divided powers

The Cartan prolong of
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
acts faithfully on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=m|n
, can be embedded into the superalgebra of polynomial vector fields of
m
even and
n
odd indeterminates, i.e., into
{\mathfrak{der}}\ {\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{a}]
(where
a=m+n
, the first
m
indeterminates are even, and the rest are odd), see
[
Shch
]
.

Over a field
{\mathbb{K}}
of characteristic
p>0
, if one tries to follow the recipe of Section
1.6
naively and use derivations of usual polynomials, instead of divided powers, it would not work. For example, let us consider the prolong
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}=1|0
, and the action of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is non-trivial. It has the form
\bigoplus\limits_{i=-1}^{\infty}{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}=1|0
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}=[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{i+1}]
for all
i\geq-1
.

The corresponding prolong over
{\mathbb{C}}
would be embedded isomorphically into
{\mathfrak{der}}\ {\mathbb{C}}[x]
so that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
would be mapped into
\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits\big(x^{i+1}\partial_{x}\big)
. Over
{\mathbb{K}}
, the construction of embedding would fail for
{\mathfrak{g}}_{p-1}
, because
[\partial_{x},x^{p}\partial_{x}]=0
, and there is no element
X
such that
[\partial_{x},X]=x^{p-1}\partial_{x}
.

However, over
{\mathbb{K}}
, Cartan prolongs can be embedded into the superalgebra of derivations of the algebra of divided powers. Let us first say a bit about these derivations.

Over
{\mathbb{C}}
, consider the action of derivation
\partial_{x_{i}}
of
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{a}]
in the basis of divided powers. It is given by (recall the definition (
1.6
) of
u^{(r)}
)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}u^{(\underline{r})}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if~~}r_{i}=0,\\
(-1)^{\max(0,i-m-1)}u^{((r_{1},\dots,r_{i-1},r_{i}-1,r_{i+1},\dots,r_{a}))}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(1.10)

Since all the coefficients are integer, the map given by this formula is a derivation of
{\mathbb{K}}[m;\underline{N}|n]
. We will denote this map
\partial_{i}:=\partial_{x_{i}}
and call the maps
\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{a}

distinguished
partial derivatives.

The
general Lie algebra of vector fields

consists of the following derivations expressed in terms of
distinguished
partial derivatives

{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)=\left\{\sum_{1\leq i\leq a}f_{i}\partial_{i}\,|\,f_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}(m;\underline{N}|n)\right\}.

Note that if
\underline{N}\neq\mathbbmss{1}
, then
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)
is not the whole
{\mathfrak{der}}\,{\mathbb{K}}[m;\underline{N}|n]
. Maps
\partial_{i}^{p^{k}}
, where
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq k<N_{i}
, are also derivations of
{\mathbb{K}}[m;\underline{N}|n]
, and a general derivation of
{\mathbb{K}}[m;\underline{N}|n]
has the form
2
2

2

It is easy to see that
{\mathbb{K}}[m;\underline{N}|n]
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{K}}[\sum N_{i};\mathbbmss{1}|n]
, so their algebras of derivations are isomorphic as well, so it is not surprising that a general element of
{\mathfrak{der}}\,{\mathbb{K}}[m;\underline{N}|n]
has
\sum N_{i}+n
functional parameters.

\sum\limits_{1\leq i\leq m}\ \sum\limits_{0\leq k\leq N_{i}-1}f_{i,k}\partial_{i}^{p^{k}}+\sum\limits_{m+1\leq i\leq a}f_{i}\partial_{i},\quad\text{where $f_{i,k},f_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}}[m;\underline{N}|n]$.}

The Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)
and its subalgebras, are called
vectorial
Lie
superalgebras (cf. with
matrix
or
linear
Lie superalgebras). Cartan prolongs can be embedded into
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)
; in particular, the above Cartan prolong would be isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1;\underline{N}_{\infty}|0)
, with
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
corresponding to
\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits\big(u_{1}^{(i+1)}\partial_{1}\big)
.

1.7.1
Notation, again

Hereafter, the symbol
{\mathfrak{g}}(a|b)
or
{\mathfrak{g}}(a;\underline{N}|b)
will designate the vectorial Lie superalgebra
with given name
{\mathfrak{g}}
realized by vector fields on the linear supermanifold
{\mathcal{K}}^{a|b}
(the one corresponding to the superspace
{\mathbb{K}}^{a|b}
, see
[
KLLS
]
), and endowed with a W-grading, see Table (
2.18
). The
standard
grading is taken as a point of reference for regradings governed
by the vector
\vec{r}
of degrees, which often can be described by one number
r
that usually (for details, see
[
LS1
,
Sh14
]
)
is equal to the number of odd indeterminates of degree 0. The regraded
Lie superalgebra is denoted by
{\mathfrak{g}}(a|b;r)
. In the standard grading
the parameter
r
is usually omitted, see Table (
2.18
) and tables in Section
.

The module
{\mathcal{F}}
of ‘‘functions’’ over
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)
and its
subalgebras (usually with the same negative part) is an analog of the

tautological
module
V
over
{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
and its subalgebras.

1.7.2
Names

The Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1;\underline{N})
is called a
Zassenhaus algebra
. For
p=2
it is not simple. Observe that

{\mathfrak{vect}}(1;\underline{N})\simeq{\mathfrak{k}}(1;\underline{N})
(indeed,
f\partial_{x}\longleftrightarrow K_{f}
, see definition (
2.2
); clearly,
\partial_{x}
is the distinguished derivative with respect to the only indeterminate). The simple derived algebra
{\mathfrak{vect}}^{(1)}(1;\underline{N})\simeq{\mathfrak{k}}^{(1)}(1;\underline{N})
is also called a
Zassenhaus algebra
causing confusion, while
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1;\mathbbmss{1})
is lately called (even for
p=0
) the

Witt algebra
in honor of Witt who was the first to study one of its modular incarnations, see Introduction to the first volume of
[
S
]
.

In the old literature,
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N})
, like its version for
p=0
, was called
the general Lie algebra of Cartan type
; lately, it is called the
Jacobson–Witt algebra
, whereas the name
Witt algebra
is reserved for the particular case
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1;\underline{1})
for
p>2
.

1.8
Traces and divergencies on vectorial Lie superalgebras

On any Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
over a supercommutative superalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}
, e.g., over a field

{\mathcal{C}}={\mathbb{K}}
, a
trace
is any linear mapping
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits\colon{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}
such that

\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}\big)=0.

(1.11)

Now let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded vectorial Lie (super)algebra with

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}:=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{i<0}{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
generated by

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
, and let
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits
be a trace on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
. Recall that any

{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of a given vectorial Lie (super)algebra is given by degrees
of the indeterminates, so the space of functions
{\mathcal{F}}
, see equation (
1.9
), is also

{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded.

The
divergence

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits\colon{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}
is a degree-preserving

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}}
-invariant extension of the trace to the Cartan prolong; this extension should satisfy the following conditions,
so
\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits\in Z^{1}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathcal{F}})
, i.e., is a cocycle:

\displaystyle X_{i}(\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits D)=\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits([X_{i},D])\text{~~for all elements $X_{i}$ that span ${\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}$},

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}}=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits,

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}}=0.

We denote by
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(u;\underline{N})
or simply

\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{u}:={\mathcal{F}}^{*}
the
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)
-module of
volume forms
dual to
{\mathcal{F}}
over
{\mathcal{F}}
. As an
{\mathcal{F}}
-module,
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{u}
is generated by the
volume element

\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{u}=1^{*}
with
fixed indeterminates (“coordinates”)
u
which we often do not
indicate. On the rank-1
{\mathcal{F}}
-module of
weighted

\lambda
-densities
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(m;\underline{N}|n)
with generator

\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{u}^{\lambda}
over
{\mathcal{F}}
, the
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)
-action is given for any
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
and
D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)
by the

Lie derivative

L_{D}\big(f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{u}^{\lambda}\big)=\big(D(f)+(-1)^{p(f)p(D)}\lambda f\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits(D)\big)\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{u}^{\lambda}.

(1.12)

The
special
Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{g}}:=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits
of
divergence-free
elements of

{\mathfrak{g}}
is the Cartan prolong of
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})
. For example,

{\mathfrak{svect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)},{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n))_{*,\underline{N}}
.

The
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
-module
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(0|n)
contains a submodule
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n)
of
codimension 1:

\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n):=\left\{f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits\,|\,\int f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits=0,\ \text{where $f\in{\mathcal{F}}=\Lambda(n)$}\right\},

(1.13)

where the
Berezin integral

\int f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{\theta}
is equal to the coefficient of the monomial of the highest in
\theta
s degree.

Over
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n)
, we often identify
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n)
with a submodule of
{\mathcal{F}}
and omit
(0|n)
; set

T_{0}^{0}(0|n):=\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n)/{\mathbb{K}}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits.

1.8.1
Examples of several divergences

On
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)
, the explicit expression of the
standard
divergence is as follows

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits\colon\ \sum f_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}\longrightarrow\sum(-1)^{p(x_{i})p(f_{i})}\partial_{x_{i}}f_{i}.

(1.14)

The supertrace restricted from
{\mathfrak{gl}}
vanishes on
{\mathfrak{q}}
, but there is an “indigenous” queer trace on
{\mathfrak{q}}
; analogously, the standard divergence (
1.14
) vanishes on certain Lie subsuperalgebras of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m;\underline{N}|n)
on which there might be defined an “indigenous” divergence.
This happens, e.g., with
{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|2n+2)
and
{\mathfrak{m}}(n)
as will be shown later on.

If there are several traces on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
, and
hence divergences on
{\mathfrak{g}}=({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{\ast,\underline{N}}
, there are several types of special
subalgebras, and we need an individual name for each.

If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a Lie
super
algebra, then the linear functional
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits

satisfying condition (
1.11
) is often called, for emphasis,

super
trace and denoted by
\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits
. If we were consistent, we
should, accordingly, use the term
super
divergence but
instead we drop the preface “super” in both cases.

1.9
Critical coordinates and unconstrained shearing vectors

The coordinate of the shearing vector
\underline{N}

corresponding to an even indeterminate of the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded vectorial Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
is said to
be
critical
if it cannot take an arbitrarily big value.

The shearing vector without any imposed restrictions on its
coordinates is said to be
unconstrained
; we denote
it by
\underline{N}^{u}
. Let

\displaystyle\text{$\dim\underline{N}$}\ \text{be the number of coordinates of $\underline{N}$},

\displaystyle\text{$\mathop{\mathrm{{P\hskip-1.29167pta\hskip-0.21529ptr\/}}}\nolimits\underline{N}^{u}$}:=\dim\underline{N}^{u}-\mathop{\mathrm{card}}\nolimits(\{\text{critical coordinates})\}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\text{$\mathop{\mathrm{{P\hskip-1.29167pta\hskip-0.21529ptr\/}}}\nolimits\underline{N}^{u}$} :=}{}\text{be the number of parameters $\underline{N}^{u}$ depends on}.

We established the (non)critical
coordinates of the shearing vectors of the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded vectorial
Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
with a computer’s aid by
explicitly computing the bases of the first several
terms
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
for
i\geq 0
without imposing any constraints on
\underline{N}
.

Conjecture 1.1
.

If the value of the coordinate
\underline{N}_{i}

(of the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded vectorial Lie
(super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
)
can be
>1
, then it can take any value.

2
Background continued. Subtleties

2.1
The serial simple vectorial Lie superalgebras over
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{C}}}
as prolongs

When we only need the vectorial Lie
superalgebras considered as abstract, not realized by vector fields,
we may consider their simplest filtrations with the smallest
codimension of their maximal subalgebras, and gradings associated with
such filtrations.

2.1.1
Convention: on central element
\boldsymbol{z\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}}

We chose the central element
z\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}

so that it acts on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
as
i\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
. The irreducible 1-dimensional module over the commutative Lie algebra spanned by
z
which acts as
i\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
is denoted by
{\mathbb{K}}[i]
.

2.2
The two types of superizations of the contact series over
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{C}}}

The type
{\mathfrak{k}}
: Define the Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{hei}}(2n|m)
on the direct sum of a
(2n,m)
-dimensional
superspace
W
, endowed with a non-degenerate antisymmetric bilinear
form
B
, and a
(1,0)
-dimensional space spanned by
z
. Clearly,
we have

{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)=({\mathfrak{hei}}(2n|m),{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{osp}}(m|2n))_{*}

and, given
{\mathfrak{hei}}(2n|m)
and a subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of

{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{osp}}(m|2n)
, we call
({\mathfrak{hei}}(2n|m),{\mathfrak{g}})_{*}
the

k
-prolong
of
(W,{\mathfrak{g}})
, where
W
is the tautological

{\mathfrak{osp}}(m|2n)
-module.

The type
{\mathfrak{m}}
: The “odd” analog of
{\mathfrak{k}}
is
associated with the following “odd” analog of
{\mathfrak{hei}}(2n|m)
.
Denote by
{\mathfrak{ba}}(n)
the
antibracket
Lie superalgebra
(
{\mathfrak{ba}}
is Anti-Bracket read backwards). Its space is
W\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\cdot z
, where
W
is an
n|n
-dimensional superspace endowed
with a non-degenerate antisymmetric odd bilinear form
B
; the
bracket in
{\mathfrak{ba}}(n)
is given by the following relations:

z
is odd and lies in the center;
[v,w]=B(v,w)\cdot z
for any
v,w\in W
.

Given
{\mathfrak{ba}}(n)
and a subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of

{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{pe}}(n)
, we call
({\mathfrak{ba}}(n),{\mathfrak{g}})_{*}
the

m
-prolong
of
(W,{\mathfrak{g}})
, where
W
is the tautological

{\mathfrak{pe}}(n)
-module.

2.3
Generating functions over
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{C}}}

A laconic way
of describing
{\mathfrak{k}}
,
{\mathfrak{m}}
and their subalgebras is via generating functions.

On the
2n+1|m
-dimensional superspace
with even coordinates
t
, and
p=(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})
,
q=(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})
, and odd indeterminates (“coordinates”)
(\xi,\eta,\theta)
, where
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k})
,
\eta=(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{k})
and
\theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{s})
, the
odd contact form
\alpha_{1}
is defined to be

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}=dt+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}(p_{i}dq_{i}-q_{i}dp_{i})+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq j\leq k}(\xi_{j}d\eta_{j}+\eta_{j}d\xi_{j})+\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\ m=2k,\\
\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq\ell\leq s}\theta_{\ell}d\theta_{\ell}&\text{if}\ m=2k+s.\end{cases}\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu

(2.1)

For any
f\in{\mathbb{C}}[t,p,q,\xi,\eta,\theta]
, set

K_{f}=(2-E)(f){\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}}+H_{f}+{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}}E,

(2.2)

where
E=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i}y_{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{y_{i}}}}
(here the
y_{i}

are all the coordinates except
t
) is the
Euler
operator
, and

H_{f}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\left({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{p_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{q_{i}}}}-{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{p_{i}}}}\right)-(-1)^{p(f)}\left(\mathop{\sum}\limits_{\ell\leq s}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\theta_{\ell}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\theta_{\ell}}}}+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{j\leq k}\left({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\xi_{j}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\eta_{j}}}}+{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\eta_{j}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{j}}}}\right)\right).

The
Hamiltonian vector
field

H_{f}
with Hamiltonian
f
preserves the symplectic form
\omega_{0}:=d\alpha_{1}
.

On the
(n|n+1)
-dimensional
superspace with even coordinates
q=(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})
, and odd indeterminates (“coordinates”)

\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
, and
\tau
, the
even contact form
\alpha_{0}
, is defined to be

\alpha_{0}=d\tau+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}(\xi_{i}dq_{i}+q_{i}d\xi_{i}).

(2.3)

For any
f\in{\mathbb{C}}[q,\xi,\tau]
, set

\displaystyle M_{f}=(2-E)(f)\partial_{\tau}-\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{f}-(-1)^{p(f)}\partial_{\tau}(f)E,\quad\text{where $E=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i}y_{i}\partial_{y_{i}}$ and
where $y=(q,\xi)$},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{f}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\left({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}\ {\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}+(-1)^{p(f)}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}\ {\frac{\partial}{\partial{q_{i}}}}\right).

Since

\displaystyle L_{K_{f}}(\alpha_{1})=2{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}}\alpha_{1}=K_{1}(f)\alpha_{1},

\displaystyle L_{M_{f}}(\alpha_{0})=-(-1)^{p(f)}2{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\tau}}}\alpha_{0}=-(-1)^{p(f)}M_{1}(f)\alpha_{0}.

(2.4)

Let
{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)
be the (contact) Lie superalgebra preserving the distribution given by the Pfaff equation with the 1-form
\alpha_{1}
; let

{\mathfrak{m}}(n):={\mathfrak{m}}(n+1|n)
be the pericontact (“odd” contact) Lie superalgebra preserving the distribution given by the Pfaff equation with the 1-form
\alpha_{0}
.

Equation (
2.4
) implies that
K_{f}\in{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)
and
M_{f}\in{\mathfrak{m}}(n)
. Observe that

p(\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{f})=p(M_{f})=p(f)+{\bar{1}}.

Contact brackets, Poisson bracket, antibracket a.k.a. Buttin (Schouten) bracket.
To the
(super)commutators
[K_{f},K_{g}]
and
[M_{f},M_{g}]
there correspond

contact brackets
of the generating functions:

\displaystyle[K_{f},K_{g}]=K_{\{f,\;g\}_{\rm k.b.}},

\displaystyle[M_{f},M_{g}]=M_{\{f,\;g\}_{\rm m.b.}}.

The explicit expressions for the contact brackets are as follows.
Let us first define the brackets on functions that do not depend on
t
(resp.
\tau
).

The
Poisson bracket

\{-,-\}_{\rm P.b.}
(in the
realization with the form

\omega_{0}:=d\alpha_{1}
for

m=2k+1
it is given by the formula

\displaystyle\{f,g\}_{\rm P.b.}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\left({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{p_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}-{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{p_{i}}}}\right)

\displaystyle{}-(-1)^{p(f)}\left(\mathop{\sum}\limits_{\ell\leq s}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\theta_{\ell}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\theta_{\ell}}}}+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{j\leq k}\left(\displaystyle{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\xi_{j}}}}{\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{\eta_{j}}}}+{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\eta_{j}}}}{\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{\xi_{j}}}}\right)\right)\quad\text{for any }f,g\in{\mathbb{C}}[p,q,\xi,\eta,\theta].

The
Buttin
3
3

3

C. Buttin was the first to publish that
the Schouten bracket satisfies super Jacobi identity.
bracket

\{-,-\}_{\rm B.b.}
, discovered by Schouten and initially
known as the
Schouten bracket
, is very popular in physics
under the name
antibracket
, see
[
GPS
]
. It is given by
the formula

\displaystyle\{f,g\}_{\rm B.b.}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\bigg({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}+(-1)^{p(f)}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}\bigg)\quad\text{for any }f,g\in{\mathbb{C}}[q,\xi].

(2.5)

In terms of the Poisson and Buttin brackets, respectively, the
contact brackets are as follows:

\displaystyle\{f,g\}_{\rm k.b.}=(2-E)(f){\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{t}}}-{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}}(2-E)(g)-\{f,g\}_{\rm P.b.},

\displaystyle\{f,g\}_{\rm m.b.}=(2-E)(f){\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{\tau}}}+(-1)^{p(f)}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\tau}}}(2-E)(g)-\{f,g\}_{\rm B.b.}.

(2.6)

The Lie superalgebras of
Hamiltonian vector
fields
(or
Hamiltonian
superalgebras
) and their special subalgebras (defined only if
n=0
)
are

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathfrak{h}}(2n|m)=\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(2n|m)\,|\,L_{D}\omega_{0}=0,\text{~~where
$\omega_{0}:=d\alpha_{1}$}\},\\
&{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}(0|m)=\left\{H_{f}\in{\mathfrak{h}}(0|m)\,|\,\int f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits=0\right\}.\end{split}

The “odd” analogues of the Lie superalgebra of Hamiltonian fields
are the Lie superalgebra of vector fields
\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{f}
introduced in

[
Le2
]
, and its special subalgebra:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{le}}(n)=\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|n)\,|\,L_{D}\omega_{1}=0,\ \text{where $\omega_{1}:=d\alpha_{0}$}\},

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{sle}}(n)=\{D\in{\mathfrak{le}}(n)\,|\,\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits D=0\}.

(2.7)

It is not difficult to prove the following isomorphisms as Lie
superalgebras with the brackets on the right-hand sides given by the above-described brackets k.b. (where
K_{f}
and
H_{f}
are involved) and m.b. (where
M_{f}
and
Le_{f}
are involved)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)=\{K_{f}\,|\,f\in{\mathbb{C}}[t,p,q,\xi]\}\cong{\mathbb{C}}[t,p,q,\xi],

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{po}}(2n|m):=\left\{K_{f}\,|\,f\in{\mathbb{C}}[t,p,q,\xi]\ \text{such that}\ {\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}}=0\right\}\cong{\mathbb{C}}[p,q,\xi],

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}(2n|m)=\{H_{f}\,|\,f\in{\mathbb{C}}[p,q,\xi]\}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[p,q,\xi]/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{m}}(n)=\{M_{f}\,|\,f\in{\mathbb{C}}[\tau,q,\xi]\}\cong{\mathbb{C}}[\tau,q,\xi],

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{b}}(n):=\left\{M_{f}\,|\,f\in{\mathbb{C}}[\tau,q,\xi),\ \text{such that}\ {\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\tau}}}=0\right\}\cong\Pi({\mathbb{C}}[q,\xi]),

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{le}}(n)=\{\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{f}\,|\,f\in{\mathbb{C}}[q,\xi]\}\cong\Pi({\mathbb{C}}[q,\xi]/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1).

We have

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{po}}^{(1)}(0|m)=\left\{K_{f}\in{\mathfrak{po}}(0|m)\,|\,\int f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits=0\right\},

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}(0|m)={\mathfrak{po}}^{(1)}(0|m)/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot K_{1}.

2.3.1
Generating functions over
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{K}}}

Recall that the contact Lie
superalgebra
{\mathfrak{k}}(n_{\bar{0}}+1;\underline{N}|n_{\bar{1}})
consists of the vector fields
D
that preserve the
contact structure (a non-integrable distribution given by a contact
form
\alpha_{1}
, cf. (
2.1
)) on the supervariety
{\mathcal{M}}

associated with the superspace
{\mathbb{K}}^{n_{\bar{0}}+1|n_{\bar{1}}}
:

\displaystyle L_{D}(\alpha_{1})=F_{D}\alpha_{1}\ \text{for some $F_{D}\in{\mathcal{F}}$,
where ${\mathcal{F}}$ is the space of functions on ${\mathcal{M}}$.}

(2.8)

Consider the form (here
n_{\bar{0}}=2k_{\bar{0}}
; if
n_{\bar{0}}
is odd, no contact
form exists)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}=dx_{0}+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}x_{i}dx_{k+i}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\alpha_1=}{}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if
$n=(n_{\bar{0}}+1)+n_{\bar{1}}=(2k_{\bar{0}}+1)+2k_{\bar{1}}=2k+1$},\\
x_{2k+1}dx_{2k+1}&\text{if $n=(n_{\bar{0}}+1)+n_{\bar{1}}=(2k_{\bar{0}}+1)+(2k_{\bar{1}}+1)=2k+2$},\end{cases}

(2.9)

where in order to make expressions for brackets simpler, we consider the following nonstandard order of indeterminates
the constituents of dual pairs one above/under the other:

\begin{array}[]{@{}llll}\text{even}\colon\ x_{0},&\text{even}\colon\ x_{1},\phantom{k_{\bar{0}}+k}\dots,x_{k_{\bar{0}}},&\text{odd}\colon\ x_{k_{\bar{0}}+1},\dots,x_{k_{\bar{0}}+k_{\bar{1}}},&\\
&\text{even}\colon\ x_{k_{\bar{0}}+k_{\bar{1}}+1},\dots,x_{2k_{\bar{0}}+k_{\bar{1}}},&\text{odd}\colon\ x_{2k_{\bar{0}}+k_{\bar{1}}+1},\dots,x_{2k};&\text{odd}\colon\ x_{2k+1}.\end{array}

The vector fields
D
satisfying (
2.8
) for some function

F_{D}
look differently for different characteristics:

For
p\neq 2
, and also if
p=2
and
n=2k+1
, the fields
D

satisfying (
2.8
) have, for any
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
, the following form
(compare with (
2.2
)):

\displaystyle K_{f}=(1-E^{\prime})(f)\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{0}}}}+{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x_{0}}}}E^{\prime}+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq k_{\bar{0}}}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{k+i}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{k+i}}}}\right)

(2.10)

\displaystyle-(-1)^{\Pi(f)}\displaystyle\left(\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k_{\bar{0}}+1\leq i\leq k}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{k+i}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{k+i}}}}\right)+\begin{cases}0&\text{if
$n=2k+1$}\\
\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{2k+1}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{2k+1}}}}&\text{if
$n=2k+2$}\end{cases}\right),

where

E^{\prime}:=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if $n=2k+1$},\\
\frac{1}{2}x_{2k+1}\partial_{x_{2k+1}}&\text{if $n=2k+2$}.\end{cases}

For
p=2
and
n=2k+2
, we cannot use formula (
2.10
)
anymore (at least, not for arbitrary
f
) since it contains

\frac{1}{2}
. In this case, the elements of the contact algebra are of
the following three types, and their linear combinations, where
k=k_{\bar{0}}+k_{\bar{1}}
:

a) For any
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that
{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x_{0}}}}={\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x_{2k+1}}}}=0
,
we have

K_{f}=(1+E^{\prime})(f){\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{0}}}}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{k+i}}}}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{k+i}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}}\right),

(2.11)

where

E^{\prime}:=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}x_{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}}
.

Observe
that in (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) we can also take
E^{\prime}:=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k+1\leq i\leq 2k}x_{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}}
.

b) Set

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}:=\mathcal{O}(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k_{\bar{0}}},x_{k+1},\dots,x_{k+k_{\bar{0}}};\underline{N}|x_{k_{\bar{0}}+1},\dots,x_{k},x_{k+k_{\bar{0}}+1},\dots,x_{2k}).

(2.12)

For any
g\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
, or equivalently, for any
g\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that
{\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{x_{2k+1}}}}=0
, we set

b1)

\displaystyle A_{g}:=g\left(x_{2k+1}\partial_{x_{0}}+\partial_{x_{2k+1}}\right),

b2)

\displaystyle B_{g}:=gx_{2k+1}\partial_{x_{2k+1}}.

For the pericontact Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{m}}(n;\underline{N}|n)
the analog of the formula (
2.9
) takes the form

\alpha_{0}=dx_{0}+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}x_{i}dx_{k+i},

where the parities of indeterminates are such that
p(x_{i})=p(x_{i+k})+{\bar{1}}
; e.g., they are as follows

\text{even}\colon\ x_{1},\dots,x_{k};\qquad\text{odd}\colon\ x_{k+1},\dots,x_{2k},\text{~and~~}x_{0}.\\

Theorem 2.1

(on explicit squaring and contact brackets for
p=2
)
.

Brackets and squares of contact vector fields, and the corresponding contact brackets of generating functions, are given by formulas (
2.14
). Both the contact brackets
\{-,-\}_{\rm k.b.}
and
\{-,-\}_{\rm m.b.}
are of the shape

\displaystyle\{f,f_{1}\}={\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x_{0}}}}(1+E^{\prime})(f_{1})+(1+E^{\prime})(f){\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}}+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}\left({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{x_{k+i}}}}+{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x_{k+i}}}}{\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}}\right),

\displaystyle\text{where~~$E^{\prime}:=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}x_{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}}$ or $E^{\prime}:=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k+1\leq i\leq 2k}x_{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}}$ for any $f,f_{1}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}$}.

(2.13)

Then, for any
f,f_{1},g,g_{1}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
, we deduce

\displaystyle[K_{f},K_{f_{1}}]=K_{\{f,f_{1}\}_{\rm k.b.}},\qquad

\displaystyle[M_{f},M_{f_{1}}]=M_{\{f,f_{1}\}_{\rm m.b.}},

\displaystyle(K_{f})^{2}=K_{\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x_{k+i}}}}},\qquad

\displaystyle(M_{f})^{2}=M_{\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x_{k+i}}}}},

\displaystyle[K_{f},A_{g}]=A_{\{f,g\}_{\rm k.b.}},\qquad

\displaystyle(A_{g})^{2}=B_{g{\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{x_{0}}}}}+g^{2}K_{1},

\displaystyle[K_{f},B_{g}]=B_{\{f,g\}_{\rm k.b.}},\qquad

\displaystyle[A_{g},B_{g_{1}}]=A_{gg_{1}},

\displaystyle[A_{g},A_{g_{1}}]=B_{{\frac{\partial{(gg_{1})}}{\partial{x_{0}}}}},\qquad

\displaystyle[B_{g},B_{g_{1}}]=(B_{g})^{2}=0.

(2.14)

Proof
is based on direct computations, see formulas
(
2.14
).

∎

In particular, for
{\mathfrak{k}}(1;\underline{N}|0)
, we have (unless
a=b=0
)

\{x^{(a)},x^{(b)}\}=\left(\Big(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{a+b-1}{a-1}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{a+b-1}{a-1}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{a+b-1}{a-1}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{a+b-1}{a-1}$}}\Big)+\Big(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{a+b-1}{b-1}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{a+b-1}{b-1}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{a+b-1}{b-1}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{a+b-1}{b-1}$}}\Big)\right)x^{(a+b-1)}=\Big(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{a+b}{a}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{a+b}{a}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{a+b}{a}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{a+b}{a}$}}\Big)x^{(a+b-1)}.

Lemma 2.2

(A helpful lemma)
.

For any
g\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
, see (
2.12
), we have
g^{2}\in{\mathbb{K}}
, see the expressions for
(A_{g})^{2}
and
[A_{g},B_{g}]
in equation (
2.14
).

Proof.

Indeed,
g=\sum_{r}g_{r}x^{(r)}
, where
r
is a
(2k+1)
-tuple of non-negative numbers
and the sum runs over a set of such tuples, and
g_{r}\in{\mathbb{K}}
for all
r
. Then,

g\cdot g=\sum_{r,s}g_{r}g_{s}x^{(r)}\cdot x^{(s)},

i.e., the terms with
r\neq s
, are encountered 2 times, so what remains is

\sum_{r}g_{r}^{2}x^{(r)}\cdot x^{(r)}=\sum_{r}g_{r}^{2}\Big(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{2r}{r}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{2r}{r}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{2r}{r}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{2r}{r}$}}\Big)x^{(2r)}=\sum_{r}g_{r}^{2}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{2k}\Big(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{2r_i}{r_i}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{2r_i}{r_i}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{2r_i}{r_i}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{2r_i}{r_i}$}}\Big)\right)x^{(2r)}.

(2.15)

If
a>0
, then
\Big(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{2a}{a}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{2a}{a}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{2a}{a}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{2a}{a}$}}\Big)=0\pmod{2}
.
By definition,
\Big(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{0}{1}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{0}{1}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{0}{1}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{0}{1}$}}\Big):=0
; hence the product in equation (
2.15
) vanishes even if there are only even indeterminates involved. Therefore, only the summand with
r=(0,\dots,0)
, i.e.,
(g_{(0,\dots,0)})^{2}
, survives. ∎

Claim 2.3

(Grading operators in
{\mathfrak{k}}(2n_{\bar{0}}+1;\underline{N}|m)
and
{\mathfrak{m}}(n;\underline{N}|n+1)
)
.

For
p\neq 2
and the bracket (
2.13
), the
x_{0}
-action gives
a grading of
{\mathfrak{g}}
by the formula
{\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{x_{0}}|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}}=i\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
; this action also
defines the
1
-dimensional
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module we denote by
{\mathbb{K}}[i]
.

For
p=2
, let

{\mathbb{K}}[*]\text{~~denote the ${\mathbb{K}}x_{0}$-module analogous to
${\mathbb{K}}[i]$};\ \ \Phi:=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}x_{i}x_{k+i}.

(2.16)

For
p=2
, the element
x_{0}
annihilates a subspace

\textrm{ann}(x_{0})|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
and acts as
multiplication by
1
on both

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}/(\textrm{ann}(x_{0})|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}})
, and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}
.

For
p=2
, the operators
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{x_{0}}|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}}
and

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{\Phi}|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}}
interchange their roles as compared with
p\neq 2
: now
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{\Phi}
commutes
with
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
.

Claim 2.4

(brackets in
{\mathfrak{k}}(2k_{\bar{0}}+1;\underline{N}|2k_{\bar{1}}+1)
)
.

In items
1)
–
3)
the isomophisms are between the LHS, defined as the Lie superalgebras of vector fields that multiply the contact form
\alpha
by a function, and the RHS, the brackets and squarings in which is given by formulas (
2.14
).

1)

{\mathfrak{k}}(1;\underline{N}|0)\simeq{\mathfrak{vect}}(1;\underline{N}|0)
.

2)

{\mathfrak{k}}(1;\underline{N}|1)\simeq({\mathbb{K}}K_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}(x_{0};\underline{N}))\bigoplus\Pi({\mathcal{O}}(x_{0};\underline{N}))
. The even part of the simple Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{k}}^{(1)}(1;\underline{N}|1)
is solvable.
(For other examples of the same phenomenon indigenous to
p=2
, see
[
BGL1
, Section
16.2
]
.)
This Lie superalgebra is the superization of
{\mathfrak{vect}}^{(1)}(1;\underline{N+1})
by “method
2
”, see
[
BLLS2
]
and
[
KrLe
]
, and the
desuperization

\textbf{F}({\mathfrak{k}}^{(1)}(1;\underline{N}))
is simple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{vect}}^{(1)}(1;\underline{N+1})
.

3)

{\mathfrak{k}}(2k_{\bar{0}}+1;\underline{N}|2k_{\bar{1}}+1)\simeq\big({\mathfrak{po}}(2k_{\bar{0}};\hat{}\underline{N}|2k_{\bar{1}})\oplus\widehat{\mathcal{F}}\big)\bigoplus\Pi\big(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}\big)
, where
\underline{N}=(N_{0},\hat{}\underline{N})
and at least one of
k_{\bar{0}}
and
k_{\bar{1}}
is non-zero. These Lie superalgebras and their desuperizations are not simple, the ideal
{\mathfrak{i}}
is generated by the
A_{g}
and
B_{g}
; recall (
2.12
). We have

{\mathfrak{k}}(2k_{\bar{0}}+1;\underline{N}|2k_{\bar{1}}+1)/{\mathfrak{i}}\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}(2k_{\bar{0}};\underline{N}|2k_{\bar{1}}).

2.4
Weisfeiler gradings

For vectorial Lie
superalgebras, the invariant notion is filtration, not grading. In characteristic

0
, the
Weisfeiler
filtrations
were used in the description
of the infinite-dimensional Lie (super)algebras
{\mathcal{L}}
by selecting a
maximal subalgebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
of finite codimension; for the simple
vectorial Lie algebra, there is only one such
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
. (Dealing
with finite-dimensional algebras for
p>0
, we can confine ourselves
to maximal subalgebras of
least
codimension, or “almost
least”.)

Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{-1}
be a minimal
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
-invariant subspace strictly
containing
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
; for
i\geq 1
, set:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{-i-1}=\begin{cases}[{\mathcal{L}}_{-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{-i}]+{\mathcal{L}}_{-i}&\text{unless $p=2$ and $i=1$},\\
[{\mathcal{L}}_{-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{-i}]+{\mathcal{L}}_{-i}+\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits\big(X^{2}\,|\,X\in{\mathcal{L}}_{-1}\big)&\text{for $p=2$ and $i=1$},\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{i}=\{D\in{\mathcal{L}}_{i-1}\,|\,[D,{\mathcal{L}}_{-1}]\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{i-1}\}.

We thus get a filtration:

{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}_{-d}\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{-d+1}\supset\dots\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\supset\cdots.

(2.17)

The
d
in (
2.17
) is called the
depth
of
{\mathcal{L}}
, and
of the associated
Weisfeiler-graded
Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{-d\leq i}{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
, where

{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}={\mathcal{L}}_{i}/{\mathcal{L}}_{i+1}
. We will for brevity say

W-graded
and
W-filtered
.

For the list of simple
W-graded
vectorial Lie superalgebras

{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{-d\leq i}{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
over
{\mathbb{C}}
, see

[
LS
]
reproduced in Tables
and
.

2.4.1
The
{\mathbb{Z}}
-gradings of vectorial Lie superalgebras

These gradings are defined by the vector
\vec{r}
of degrees of the indeterminates, but this
